##### <span class="small-caps">Translation.</span>

<span class="small-caps">Obv.</span> Unto [ . . . . . ]ṣu-Sin say :—

“ Thus saith Abēšu’. The chief captain[^fn-p0238-1] of Sippar-amnanu requireth (?) a ship of sixty <span class="small-caps">gur</span> capacity[^fn-p0238-2] to [convey] the corn for the palace. When thou shalt behold this tablet, let the chief captain of Sippar - amnanu [ . . . . . ] a ship of sixty <span class="small-caps">gur</span> capacity . . . . .”

<span class="small-caps">Rev.</span> “. . . . . And thou shalt see that the ship of sixty <span class="small-caps">gur</span> capacity arrive at Babylon on the twenty-fifth day of the month Sivan.”

As the letter is imperfect, the exact nature of the king’s instructions is not apparent. From the reverse of the tablet, however, it is clear that Abēšu’ required a ship of sixty <span class="small-caps">gur</span> capacity to be sent to Babylon, and from l. 6 of the obverse it may be inferred that the ship was wanted in connection with certain corn for the palace. The restoration suggested in the translation, to the effect that the vessel was required for carrying corn to the palace, is therefore not improbable.

### Notes

[^fn-p0213-1]: (1) The thirteen letters of Abēšu’ here published are, with one exception, written to men in authority in the city of Sippar, and they are here arranged according to the names of those to whom they are addressed. Nos. I–III are written to Sin-idinnam, Kār-Sippar, and the Judges of Sippar ; No. IV to Ibni-Šamaš, Sin-idinnam, Kār-Sippar, and the Judges of Sippar, i.e. to the same men as those addressed in Nos. I–III, with the addition of Ibni-Šamaš ; No. V is addressed to five men who were probably rulers of the city of Kār-Šamaš, and it is placed after Nos. I–IV as it was written in consequence of a report received by the king from Sin-idinnam, Kār-Sippar, and the Judges of Sippar. No. VI is addressed to Sin-idinnam, whom we may identify with the Sin-idinnam of Nos. I–V. Nos. VII–X are written to Marduk-naṣir, Kār-Sippar, and the Judges of Sippar. No. XI is addressed to [ . . . . . ], Šamaš-šumu[ . . . . ], Kār-Sippar, and the Judges of Sippar ; and No. XIII to [ . . . . . ]ṣu-Sin, who, to judge from the contents of the letter, was probably a high official in the same city. For an explanation of the phrase “ Kār-Sippar and the Judges of Sippar,” see above, p. 122.
[^fn-p0214-1]: (2) The Irnina-canal is mentioned in a list of canals, K. 4,337 (see *Cun. Inscr. West. Asia*, vol. ii, pl. 50, l. 15), where it is stated that its Sumerian name was <span class="small-caps">Id-ega-gal-gal-la</span>, “the canal of the great stream.” From the above letter it is clear that its ancient name was not ill-chosen. By the Semitic Babylonians the canal was renamed after the god Irnina.
[^fn-p0214-2]: (3) With this title cf. the expression *be-el bi-ḫa-tim* applied by Abēšu’ in one of his letters (No. XII) to certain merchants of Sippar.
[^fn-p0214-3]: (4) This rendering of *ṣāb bi-ir-tim* is provisional.
[^fn-p0216-1]: (1) With this title cf. the somewhat similar one, *<sup>amêlu</sup>* *ga-ab-ba-ḫu-u*, in the letter of Abēšu’, No. XI.
[^fn-p0216-2]: (2) In the combination <span class="small-caps">sag-amat-uru</span>, <span class="small-caps">uru</span> seems to be employed as a general word for “slave,” while <span class="small-caps">amat</span> indicates the gender; for the explanation of <span class="small-caps">sag</span>, see Meissner, *Altbabylonisches Privatrecht*, p. 92.
[^fn-p0216-3]: (3) On Sippar-amnanu, see above, p. 118, note 2.
[^fn-p0216-4]: (4) See above, p. 7.
[^fn-p0218-1]: (1) For this meaning of the verb *ḫabālu*, see above, p. 24, note 3. As in the letter of Ḫammurabi there referred to (No. IX), it would be possible here also to assign to *ḫabālu* its usual meaning, “to wrong, damage” ; Bunene-naṣir and Mini-Šamaš would then begin their appeal with the words “Ili-idinnam, our elder brother, hath wronged us. For two years,” etc. On the whole, however, it is preferable to assign to the word the meaning which it has elsewhere in these letters.
[^fn-p0218-2]: (2) The *Kār-Sippar* appears to have had some such meaning ; see further, p. 122, note 2.
[^fn-p0218-3]: (3) Cf. also p. 41.
[^fn-p0220-1]: (1) One of the letters of Samsu-iluna is addressed to Ibni-Marduk, Sin-idinnam, and the Judges of Sippar (see above, p. 128). It has already been suggested that the Sin-idinnam mentioned in Samsu-iluna’s letters is to be identified with Abēšu’s correspondent of the same name ; and it would be tempting to restore the name in the first line of this letter as Ibni-Marduk. The space on the tablet, however, suggests the restoration of Šamaš, rather than Marduk, as the second half of the name.
[^fn-p0220-2]: (2) For this rendering of the ideogram <span class="small-caps">u-ṭul</span> (= *utullu*), see above, p. 56 f.
[^fn-p0220-3]: (3) On Sippar-iaḫrurum, see above, p. 118, note 2.
[^fn-p0222-1]: (1) The five men addressed were clearly men in authority in the city of Kār-Šamaš.
[^fn-p0222-2]: (2) It is possible that this passage should be translated “ we wrote that we might receive two changes of raiment, etc., and that he should come unto Sippar ”; in that case l. 17 should be restored [*la ni-il-k*]*i-a-am-ma*.
[^fn-p0222-3]: (3) The ideogram is more clearly written in ll. 22 and 25; it seems to be <span class="small-caps">KU</span> (i.e. *ṣubātu*), not *buḫadu*, as in Nos. VI and VII. It is possible to read the numeral as 120.
[^fn-p0223-1]: (1) The ideogram is not very clearly written, but appears to be *buḫadu*, as in the following letter. The duties of this official evidently consisted in assessing and collecting tribute.
[^fn-p0224-1]: (2) If the verb is correctly restored as *ṣi-*[*im-da*]*m-ma* it is clear that *buḫadē* refers to the young of the herds, not to the young of the flocks.
[^fn-p0224-2]: (3) See above, p. 131, note 1.
[^fn-p0224-3]: (4) In one of his letters to Sin-idinnam Ḫammurabi refers to the tribute which was paid by Larsam to Babylon ; see above, p. 12 f.
[^fn-p0225-1]: (1) As the letter is addressed to the Judges of Sippar-amnanu and not to those of Sippar, it is possible that *Kār-Sippar**<sup>KI</sup>* should not be restored.
[^fn-p0226-1]: (2) On the various duties of this official, see above, p. 7.
[^fn-p0226-2]: (3) The restoration of this line, as suggested above, is not quite certain; the traces of I before <span class="small-caps">GIN</span> and of <span class="small-caps">UD</span> at the end of the line are not very clear.
[^fn-p0228-1]: (1) On Sippar-amnanu, see above, p. 118, note 2.
[^fn-p0228-2]: (2) With this title compare that of *Gal Martu*, applied to a certain Sin-idinnam in letter No. 48 (Miscellaneous Letters, No. II). It is possible that the signs which followed <span class="small-caps">gal</span> did not give the name of a town, but formed part of Sin-mušalim’s title, of which <span class="small-caps">gal</span> was the first syllable.
[^fn-p0228-3]: (3) For this explanation of the ideogram <span class="small-caps">šu-kaskal</span>, see above, p. 8, note 5.
[^fn-p0230-1]: (1) ¹ The *durmaḫu* was evidently a priest of high rank ; for the reading of the ideogram, see Brünnow, *Classified List*, Nos. 5,072 and 10,577.
[^fn-p0230-2]: (2) ² The verb should possibly be read *li-ip-pa-ri-i[s]* ; cf. Letter I of Ammiditana (No. 56), l. 24, *wa-ar-ka-tu l[i]-ip-ru-s[u-ma]*. In that case the reason for sending the priest to Babylon would be that the future might be divined.
[^fn-p0231-1]: (1) It is possible that *amēlu* should be taken as a determinative before a title.
[^fn-p0233-1]: (1) On Sippar-iaḫrurum, see above, p. 118, note 2.
[^fn-p0233-2]: (2) It is preferable to take this as a proper name rather than to translate “the daughter of Dungi”; for other instances of the use of royal names as component parts of proper names, see above, pp. 6 and 139.
[^fn-p0234-1]: (3) For this explanation of the phrase *Kār-Sippar* *<sup>KI</sup>* *u daianē**<sup>pl</sup>* *Sippar* *<sup>KI</sup>*, see above, p. 122. It would appear from this letter that the Court of Sippar claimed jurisdiction over the neighbouring towns and villages.
[^fn-p0234-2]: (4) This seems to be the meaning of the expression *u-ul ni-iz-zi-iz*; perhaps translate “ we were not present (at the investigation).” The local judges of Sippar-iaḫrurum clearly resented the action of the Court of Sippar.
[^fn-p0236-1]: (1) For this reading of the ideogram, see Brünnow, *Classified List*, No. 9,384 ; for another instance of its use, see above, p. 12.
[^fn-p0236-2]: (2) Cf. the similar expression *amēlē**<sup>pl</sup>* *šu-ut bi-ḫa-a-tim* in Letter I of Abēšu’, l. 12 ; see above, p. 130.
[^fn-p0238-1]: (1) Another expression for a “captain of a ship” is *akil* <span class="small-caps">ma-ni-um</span>; see above, pp. 60 f. and 64.
[^fn-p0238-2]: (2) For the system of measuring the capacity of vessels employed for transport, see above, p. 84. In other passages where the size of ships is given the ideogram <span class="small-caps">ma-ni-um</span> is followed simply by the number of <span class="small-caps">gur</span> which formed the vessel’s capacity. In this letter the substantive *ne-me-it-ti* is probably to be taken with the preceding phrase <span class="small-caps">ma-ni-um</span> 60 <span class="small-caps">gur</span>, and rendered by some such word as “capacity” or “rating.”

## IV. LETTERS OF AMMIDITANA, KING OF BABYLON

### Letter 75: Ammiditana → Marduk-mušalim / Sin-idinnam / Apil-Sin

#### I.

#### ORDER FOR THE DESPATCH OF CORN WHEN<br />THE OMENS ARE FAVOURABLE.

[Bu. 91-5-9, 340 ; pl. 95, No. 56.]

5 *um* - *ma* *Am* *mi* - *di* - *ta* - *na* - *ma* 5  
*amēlē**<sup>pl</sup>* *šu-ut bi-ḫa-a-tim ša i-na Šag-ga* *<sup>KI</sup>* *wa-aš-bu*

57 <span class="small-caps">GUR</span> 184½ <span class="small-caps">ḲA</span>

10 *ša* *<sup>arḫu</sup>* *Kislimu* 10

*<sup>v</sup>u up - ra amēlē<sup>pl</sup> Ba -* [*ab - bi - li*]  
<span class="small-caps">Edge.</span> *a-na ma-aḫ-ri-ku-nu l*[*i-il-li-ku-nim-ma*]

20 57 <span class="small-caps">GUR</span> 184½ <span class="small-caps">ḲA</span> 20  
*a-na kurmat ṣābi bi-ir-ti Šag-ga<sup>KI</sup>* [*u a-ḫi-a-tim*]  
*ša <sup>arḫu</sup> Kislimu šu - um - ḫi ra -* [*am - ma*]  
*bārūti<sup>pl</sup> ša ma - aḫ - ri -* [*ku - nu*]  
*wa - ar ka - tu l*[*i*] *- ip - ru - s*[*u - ma*]  
25 *i - na <sup>šīru</sup> te - ri - e - tim ša - al - ma - a - t*[*im*] 25  
*še - am šu - a - ti*  
*a na Šag - ga<sup>KI</sup> šu - bi la*

###### <span class="small-caps">Translation.</span>

Unto Marduk-mušalim, Sin-idinnam, and Apil-Sin  
say :—

“Thus saith Ammiditana. The men of the province[^fn-p0240-1] who dwell in the city of Šagga have written unto me, saying, ‘Fifty-seven <span class="small-caps">gur</span> and one hundred and eighty-four and a half <span class="small-caps">ḳa</span> of corn, for the food of the men of the citadel[^fn-p0240-2] in the city of Šagga, and in the district round about during the month Kislev, are lacking.’ In this fashion have they written unto me. Inasmuch as at the time when ye shall write unto the men of the province who dwell in the city of Šagga, “men of Babylon will have been sent unto you, ye shall send for the men of Babylon that they may come into your presence. And from the corn which is in their hands shall ye take fifty-seven <span class="small-caps">gur</span> and one hundred and eighty-four and a half <span class="small-caps">ḳa</span> as food for the men of the citadel in the city of Šagga and the district round about for the month Kislev. And let the soothsayers who are in your presence[^fn-p0241-1] divine the future, and (then) with favourable omens shall ye bring this corn into the city of Šagga.”

As the following letter of Ammiditana is addressed to a high official of Sippar, it is not improbable that Marduk-mušalim, Sin-idinnam, and Apil-Sin, to whom the present letter is written, also held posts in that city. In that case the city of Šagga probably lay in the neighbourhood of Sippar, and the authorities of that city would naturally be entrusted with the task of arranging the supply of corn for its needs. The chief point of interest in the letter lies in the last five lines of the reverse, in which the king gives instructions for the *bārūti*, or “soothsayers,”[^fn-p0241-2] to examine the portents in order that the corn should be brought into Šagga under favourable auspices.

### Letter 76: Ammiditana → the scribe of the merchants of Sippar-iaḫrurum

#### **II.**<br />ORDER FOR THE PAYMENT OF TRIBUTE<br />WHICH IS OVERDUE.

[Bu. 91–5–9, 325 ; pl. 93, No. 55.]

*ki - bi - ma*  
*um - ma Am - mi di ta - na - ma*  
*<sup>amēlu</sup> mu - ša ad - di - in šipāti* (?)  
5 *ki-a-am u-lam-mi-da-an-ni um-ma šu-ma* 5  
*a-na akil damḳari Sippar<sup>KI</sup>-ia-aḫ-ru-rum*

15 *a - na Bābili<sup>KI</sup>* 15  
*la tu - ša - bi - lam*  
*a - na e - bi - e - ši - im an - ni - i - im*  
*ki - i la ta - ap - la - aḫ*  
<span class="small-caps">Rev.</span> *ki - ma dub - bi an ni - a -* [*am*]  
20 *ta - am - ma - r*[*u*] 20  
*šipāta ne - me - it - ta - ka*  
*a na Bābili<sup>KI</sup>*  
*šu - bi - lam*

##### <span class="small-caps">Translation.</span>

Unto the scribe of the merchants[^fn-p0243-1] of Sippar-iaḫrurum say :—

“Thus saith Ammiditana. The assessor of wool[^fn-p0243-2] hath informed me, saying, ‘I wrote unto the scribe of the merchants of Sippar-iaḫrurum bidding him bring the wool that is due from him unto Babylon, but he hath not brought the wool that is due from him.’ After this fashion hath he informed me. Wherefore hast thou not brought unto Babylon the wool that is due from thee? Since thou hast not feared to do this thing, when thou shalt behold this tablet the wool that is due from thee shalt thou (forthwith) bring unto Babylon.”

Three letters written by Abēšu’ (Nos. V, VI, and VII) bear a very close resemblance to the present letter, for they deal with the collection of tribute which is overdue, and in each case the form of the letter is the same. In them, as in the present letter, the king writes in consequence of a report he has received to the effect that certain tribute which is due has not been delivered ; and in each case he gives an abrupt order that it shall be promptly paid.[^fn-p0243-3]

### Letter 77: Ammizaduga → Ibni-Sin

#### **I.**<br />SUMMONS TO A SHEEP-SHEARING.

[Brit. Mus., No. 17,298; pl. 85, No. 50.]

###### <span class="small-caps">Transliteration.</span>

<span class="small-caps">Obv.</span> *a - na Ib - ni - **<sup>ilu</sup>* *Si*[*n*]  
*mār **<sup>ilu</sup>* *Marduk - na ṣi ir*  
*ki - bi - m*[*a*]  
*um ma Am mi - za - du ga m*[*a*]  
5 [*b*]*u - ku - m*[*u*] 5  
[*i - na*] *bīt a - ki - tim*  
[*iš*] *ša - ak - ka - an*  
[*ki - ma d*]*ub - bi an - ni a - *[*am*]  
[ *ta*] - *am - ma - ru*  
10 [ . . . . ] *ṣēnē**<sup>pl</sup>* *ša te - ri - *[ . . . . ]  
[*ṣa*] - *ab - tu*

15 *a - na Bābili**<sup>KI</sup>* *al - kam* 15  
*la t*[*u - la*] - *ap - p*[*a*] - *tu*  
<span class="small-caps">Rev.</span> *i na **<sup>arḫu</sup>* *Addaru ūmu* 1 *<sup>KAM</sup>*

###### <span class="small-caps">Translation.</span>

Unto Ibni-Sin, the son of Marduk-naṣir, say :—

“ Thus saith Ammizaduga. A sheep-shearing[^fn-p0245-1] will take place in the House of the Feast of the New Year. When thou shalt behold this tablet, [ . . . . ] the sheep which [ . . . . ] have taken, and those of the sheep which are marked[^fn-p0245-2] and which thou shalt bring forth, shalt thou take (with thee) and come to Babylon. Thou shalt not delay, but shalt arrive at Babylon on the first day of the month Adar.”

All five letters of Ammizaduga, which are here published, are on the same subject ; they consist of a series of summonses to attend the sheep-shearing at Babylon. Nos. I–IV are addressed to Ibni-Sin, the son of Marduk-naṣir, and were evidently written in different years. It will be noticed that the date of the sheep-shearing varied slightly. Thus, in the present letter Ibni-Sin is told to reach Babylon on the first day of the month Adar ; in No. II he is told to arrive on the tenth day of the month Sebat, and in No. III on the eighth day of that month ; in Nos. IV and V the dates are wanting. As Nos. II and III are practically duplicates of the present letter, with the exception of l. 17 containing the date, the translation given above has not been repeated.

### Letter 81: Ammizaduga → [ . . . . . ]-naṣir

#### **V.**<br />SUMMONS TO A SHEEP-SHEARING.

###### <span class="small-caps">Translation</span>.

Unto [ . . . . . ]-naṣir, the [ . . . . . ], say :—

“ Thus saith Ammizaduga. A sheep-shearing will take place in the House of the Feast of the New Year. When thou shalt behold this tablet, [ . . . . ] the sheep which [ . . . . ] have taken . . . .”

The missing portion of the text may probably be restored from No. 54 (III), ll. 11–19, with the exception of the date in l. 17.

Unto the President of the Court[^fn-p0250-1] in the city of Kutalla[^fn-p0250-2] say :—

[Bu. 91–5–9, 1,081; pl. 82, No. 48.]

##### <span class="small-caps">TRANSLITERATION.</span>

*nu* - *uš* - *ta* - *bi* - *lam*

20 [ . . . . - *l*]*a* - *am* - *ki* *i* - *na* *a* - *ḫi* - *ni* 20  
[ . . . . . ] *i* *ni* - *ik* *ku* - *ur*

##### <span class="small-caps">Translation.</span>

Unto Aḫatim, the wife of Sin-idinnam, the governor of Martu,[^fn-p0252-1] (say):—

“Thus say Tabbiwadi and Mār-Šamaš, thy servants.[^fn-p0252-2] The governor of Martu hath sent us unto thee. Unto us[^fn-p0252-3] palaces are strange, but one hath taken us into the palace. Thy . . . . do we possess. Unto our father[^fn-p0252-4] mayest thou . . . . that our father may free us. We have brought the tablets unto our father. With regard to that . . . . may he show favour, and may he write unto his son, that his son (also) may free us. When we shall behold thy countenance, may we remove thine [anger (?)] towards[^fn-p0252-5] us.”

Tabbiwadi and Mār-Šamaš had in some way incurred the wrath of Sin-idinnam, and of his son and wife. Sin-idinnam had not dealt with the case himself, but had sent the two men to his wife. They had arrived at the palace, and they now presented to Aḫatim this petition for pardon. They first refer to their want of knowledge of the ways of the court, perhaps to excuse the unusual nature of their petition. They then throw themselves on Aḫatim’s mercy, seeking her intervention on their behalf with the governor and his son,[^fn-p0253-1] and ending with the request that they may secure her favour when they are brought into her presence. Some expressions in the letter are obscure, and it is possible that the writers were foreigners who had settled in Babylonia.[^fn-p0253-2]

### Notes

[^fn-p0250-1]: (1) For this meaning of the title *rabiānu*, see above, p. 38 f. It is possible that the action mentioned in the letter had come before the court of Kutalla, and had been referred to Sin-idinnam for settlement.
[^fn-p0250-2]: (2) The name of this city was also written *Ku-ta-la*.
[^fn-p0250-3]: (3) The writer of this letter may probably be identified with the Sin-idinnam of Larsam, to whom Ḫammurabi’s letters are addressed.
[^fn-p0250-4]: (4) On the rendering of the phrase *bēl amāti*, see above, p. 39, note 2.
[^fn-p0251-1]: (1) The sign is <span class="small-caps">NIK</span>, not <span class="small-caps">DAM</span>; otherwise the sentence might perhaps be transliterated *li-ib damḳari ša-tu li-ti-bu-ma*, with *li-ib* as the subject of the verb.
[^fn-p0252-1]: (2) It is quite possible that *Martu* in the phrase *Gal Martu* had a local significance, and merely referred to a district in Babylonia. On the other hand, it has been suggested that it refers, not to the country of Martu, but to men from that country, the title of *Gal Martu* being held by an official who was set over the slaves or workmen from Martu. (Cf. F. Thureau Dangin, *Revue critique*, 1899, No. 32, p. 106.)
[^fn-p0252-2]: (3) The singular is here used collectively.
[^fn-p0252-3]: (4) The word *e-li-nu-um* is taken in the translation as the preposition *eli* with the 1 pl. suffix and the mimation ; this seems preferable to regarding the word as an adverb, with the meaning “formerly, hitherto.”
[^fn-p0252-4]: (5) The expression “our father” is probably not to be taken literally, but to be regarded as referring to Sin-idinnam.
[^fn-p0252-5]: (6) In the phrase *i-na a-ḫi-ni*, *aḫu* clearly has the meaning “side.”
[^fn-p0253-1]: (7) If the expression “our father” in ll. 11, 12, and 13 refers to Sin-idinnam, it follows that *ma-ri-šu* in l. 16 is Sin-idinnam’s son.
[^fn-p0253-2]: (8) This may perhaps be inferred from the unusual form of the pronominal suffix of the 1st pers. plur. attached to the verbs in ll. 6, 8, 12, and 18. In contract-tablets of this period there is abundant evidence that foreigners settled in Babylonia and traded there.

#### <span class="small-caps">Translation.</span>

<span class="small-caps">Col. I.</span>

.       .       .       .       .       .       .       .  
Bēl hath bestowed lordly rank upon thee.  
      For whom, then, dost thou wait ?  
Sin hath invested thee with princely power.  
      For whom, then, dost thou wait ?  
Ninib hath given thee a noble weapon.  
      For whom, then, dost thou wait ?  
Ištar hath granted thee the contest and the battle.  
      For whom, then, dost thou wait ?

Šamaš and Rammān are thy guardians.  
For whom, then, dost thou wait?

##### <span class="small-caps">Col. II.</span>

.   .   .   .   .   .   .   .  
Establish thy might.  
In the four quarters of the world  .  .  .  
May thy name be proclaimed.  
May mankind in its myriads  
Address supplication unto thee.  
May they bow down their faces  
In reverence before thee.  
Let them celebrate  
Thy great glory.  
Let them tender their obedience  
Unto thy supremacy.

##### <span class="small-caps">Col. III.</span>

[Only the ends of a few lines of the Semitic Version have been preserved.]

##### <span class="small-caps">Col. IV.</span>

He hath established.[^fn-p0258-1]  
He hath made glorious for future days  
The greatness of his power—  
Ḫammurabi, the king, the strong warrior,  
The destroyer of his foes.  
He is the hurricane of the battle,  
He casteth down the land of his enemy.  
He bringeth opposition to nought,  
And he putteth an end to insurrection.  
He destroyeth the warrior  
Like an image of clay.  
And he breaketh through the barrier  
Of impassable mountains.  
.   .   .   .   .   .   .   .

##### <span class="small-caps">Sumerian Version.</span>

<span class="small-caps">LUGAL LUGAL-E NE-IR  
BA-RA AN DIM-MA  
(DINGIR) UTU LUGAL-MA  
40 GAL BI ḪU-MU-NA-RU</span>

##### <span class="small-caps">Semitic Version.</span>

*šarru in šarri ali*[^fn-p0261-1]  
*la ib ni u  
a na* *<sup>ilu</sup>* *Šamaš be-li ia*  
45 *ra bi iš  
lu e-pu-uš-su-um*

##### <span class="small-caps">Translation.</span>

Ḫammurabi, the mighty king, the king of Babylon, the king of the four quarters, the founder of the land, the king whose deeds are well-pleasing unto the heart of Šamaš and Marduk, am I.

The summit of the wall of Sippar I have raised with earth like unto a great mountain. I have compassed it about with a swamp. I have digged out the Euphrates[^fn-p0261-2] unto Sippar, and I have set up a wall of safety for it.

Ḫammurabi, the founder of the land, the king whose deeds are well-pleasing unto the heart of Šamaš and Marduk, am I. I have caused Sippar and Babylon to dwell continuously in a peaceful habitation. Ḫammurabi, the darling of Šamaš, the beloved of Marduk, am I. That which from days of old no king had built for the king of the city,[^fn-p0261-3] for Šamaš my lord I have gloriously accomplished.

##### <span class="small-caps">Translation.</span>

For Ninni of the city of Ḫallab, the lady whose splendour covereth[^fn-p0267-2] heaven and earth, his lady, Ḫammurabi, the minister of Anu, the servant[^fn-p0267-3] of Bēl, the beloved of Šamaš, the shepherd who delighteth Marduk’s heart, the prince who is dear to the heart of Ninni, the mighty king, the king of Babylon, the king of Sumēr and Akkad, the king of the four quarters of the world, the king who hath built anew the shrines of the great gods,—when Ninni, the protector of his prosperity, gave unto him Sumēr and Akkad to rule, and entrusted their sceptre to his hands, then did (Ḫammurabi) build for Ninni, who is beloved by him, the temple E-zi-kalama, the temple, which she loveth, in Ḫallab, the city of her sway.

##### <span class="small-caps">Translation.</span>

For Marduk, the great lord, the giver of abundance, for the god who is lord of E-sagil and E-zida, for his own lord, Ḫammurabi, the minister of Anu, the servant[^fn-p0269-1] of Bēl, the darling of Šamaš, the shepherd who is beloved of Marduk, the mighty king, the king of the land of Sumēr and Akkad, the king of the four quarters of the world,—when Bēl gave him the land and the people to rule, and entrusted their sceptre to his hands, for Marduk, the god who begat him, did he build E-zida, his bright temple, in Borsippa, the city which is beloved of him.

*lu u š*[*e*] - *š*[*ib*]

#### <span class="small-caps">Translation.</span>

Ḫammurabi, the mighty king, the king of Babylon, the king who hath brought to subjection the four quarters of the world, who hath brought about the triumph of Marduk, the shepherd who delighteth his heart, am I.

When Anu and Bēl gave (me) the land of Sumēr and Akkad to rule and entrusted their sceptre to my hands, I dug out the Ḫammurabi-canal (named) Nuḫuš-niši,[^fn-p0273-1] which bringeth abundance of water unto the land of Sumēr and Akkad. Both the banks thereof I changed[^fn-p0273-2] to fields for cultivation, and I garnered piles of grain, and I procured unfailing water for the land of Sumēr and Akkad.

As for the land of Sumēr and Akkad, I collected the scattered peoples thereof, and I procured food and drink for them. In abundance and plenty I pastured them, and I caused them to dwell in a peaceful habitation.

At that time I, Ḫammurabi, the mighty king, the beloved of the great gods, through the great power which Marduk had bestowed (upon me), built a lofty fortress,[^fn-p0273-3] at the head of the Ḫammurabi-canal (named) Nuḫuš-niši, with much earth, the top of which reacheth on high like unto a mountain. This fortress I named Dūr-Sin-muballiṭ-abim-walidia, (and thus) did I cause the name of Sin-muballiṭ, the father who begat me, to dwell in the (four) quarters of the world.

> *apil <sup>amēlu</sup>*<span class="small-caps">SAG-ṢAB</span> *<sup>amēlu</sup>*<span class="small-caps">ŠAGAN-LAL-NAB-TUR</span> *iš-ṭur-ma*  
> *a-na balāṭ napšāti<sup>pl</sup>-šu ṭub(ub) lib-bi-šu u šemī su-pi-e-šu*

##### <span class="small-caps">TRANSLATION.</span>

For Bēl, the great lord of heaven and earth, king of the gods, my lord, I, Ḫammurabi, the prince in whom Bēl taketh delight, the beloved shepherd of Ninib, the reverent one who showeth obedience unto Šamaš and maketh glad the heart of Marduk, the mighty king, the king of Babylon, the humble and reverent one [ . . . . ],—when Bēl gave (me) the peoples of his land to rule and set the sceptre thereof within my hands, I made (in) Babylon, his beloved city, a granary to rejoice his heart.

(This copy[^fn-p0275-1] of an inscription), which (was engraved) upon a tablet in the temple of E-namtila by Ḫammurabi, the king, Rīmūt-Gula, son of a captain of the host, a . . . . , hath written and hath placed it in E-zida, the beloved temple of Nabū, in the hope that his life may be preserved, and that his heart may be made glad, and that his prayers may be answered.

<sup>1</sup>[ . . . . ] - *bi*, <sup>2</sup>[ . . . . ] - *ku*  
<sup>3</sup>[ . . . . ] - *bi*, <sup>4</sup>[ . . . . ] - *ku*  
. . . . . . . . . . . . .

#### <span class="small-caps">Translation.</span>

. . . . Ḫammurabi, the mighty king, . . . . .[^fn-p0276-1]

### Notes

[^fn-p0254-1]: (1) It is not certain how much of the inscription is missing ; it is possible that more than one double column is wanting before both Col. i and Col. iii. The original length of the columns, which are only partly preserved, is also uncertain.
[^fn-p0254-2]: (2) For the restoration of the beginning of this line, on the basis of *Cun. Inscr. West. Asia*, vol. iv, pl. 23, No. 2, Obv., l. 7 f., see Amiaud, *Rev. d’Assyr.*, ii, p. 11.
[^fn-p0255-1]: (1) On this form of the name of the goddess <span class="small-caps">Ninni</span> or *Ištar*, cf. Amiaud, op. cit., p. 12; and for the value <span class="small-caps">muš</span> (*= zīmu*), see 83–1–18, 1,338, col. iii, l. 30 (published by Bezold, *Proc. Soc. Bibl. Arch.*, Dec., 1888, pl. ii, and cited by Jensen in Schrader’s *Keilins. Bibl.*, Bd. iii, 1, p. 112).
[^fn-p0255-2]: (2) For this restoration, cf. Brünnow, *Classified List*, No. 6,567; since <span class="small-caps">a-gal</span> = *li’ū*, “strong,” it may be inferred that <span class="small-caps">nam-a-gal</span> = *lītu*, “strength.”
[^fn-p0256-1]: (1) The meaning of this phrase is not clear. Amiaud rendered the Semitic version as *ūmi li-li-ma*, “day and night,” ignoring the Sumerian version; Jensen confines himself to the Sumerian version, and, as <span class="small-caps">an</span> = “high” and <span class="small-caps">il</span> = “to be high” and “to raise,” he conjecturally renders the whole sentence “may thy name be *loudly* proclaimed.”
[^fn-p0257-2]: (2) The value of this sign is uncertain.
[^fn-p0257-3]: (3) Jensen suggests the restoration [<span class="small-caps">me</span> (= *taḫazu*)] -<span class="small-caps">ag</span>, literally “the maker of war,” as the equivalent of *muḳtabli*.
[^fn-p0257-4]: (4) Two signs at least are wanting at the beginning of the line, which may perhaps be restored [<span class="small-caps">mu-un-gul</span>]-<span class="small-caps">gul-la</span> ; *mu’abbit* (ii, 1, *abātu*) might be expected in the Semitic version rather than *muḫabbit* (ii, 1, *ḫabātu*), and it is possible that *ḫa* is here employed merely for the breathing.
[^fn-p0257-1]: (1) There is no equivalent for *māt* (<span class="small-caps">kalam</span>) in the Sumerian version.
[^fn-p0258-1]: (1) The beginning of this sentence occurred at the end of Col. III.
[^fn-p0259-1]: (1) Var. <span class="small-caps">ḪE-IM-MI-IL</span>.
[^fn-p0259-2]: (2) Jensen (*Keilins. Bibl.*, iii, 1, p. 118) suggests the reading <span class="small-caps">APPAR</span> for the sign.
[^fn-p0259-3]: (3) Cf. Brünnow, *Classified List*, Nos. 10,328 and 10,339.
[^fn-p0260-2]: (2) Var. <span class="small-caps">DIM-DIM</span>.
[^fn-p0260-3]: (3) There is nothing wanting at the beginning of this line; the copula <span class="small-caps">BI-DA-GE</span> joins <span class="small-caps">ZIMBIR(KI)</span> and <span class="small-caps">KA-DINGIR-RA(KI)</span>.
[^fn-p0260-4]: (4) One duplicate omits this line.
[^fn-p0260-1]: (1) Var. *lu-u-uš-ta-aš-ḫi-ir-šu.*
[^fn-p0261-1]: (1) The sign <span class="small-caps">URU</span> (*ali*) is read by all the duplicates.
[^fn-p0261-2]: (2) (<span class="small-caps">ID</span>)<span class="small-caps">UD-KIB-NUN</span> is probably an abbreviation of (<span class="small-caps">ID</span>)<span class="small-caps">UD-KIB-NUN-KI</span>, the Euphrates.
[^fn-p0261-3]: (3) That is, “for his city-god”; the Sumerian version reads <span class="small-caps">LUGAL-E-NE-IR</span>, “for his kings,” i.e. his gods.
[^fn-p0263-1]: (1) It is possible that <span class="small-caps">si-a</span> should be taken as a single sign; cf. Brünnow, *Classified List*, No. 3,739.
[^fn-p0263-2]: (2) The value of the sign <span class="small-caps">ši + um</span> is unknown; here and in No. 61, l. 26, it is probably the equivalent of *tukultu*, “help” (i.e. “helper, protector”), in which sense the sign occurs in proper names.
[^fn-p0264-1]: (3) Three of the phrases in ll. 5–11 correspond to those in the Semitic inscription No. 94, ll. 10–17, which read : *na-bi-u An-nim* [ . . . ]*-mu* *<sup>ilu</sup>* *Bēl* [*mi*]*-gi-ir* *<sup>ilu</sup>* *Šamaš rē’ū na-ra-am* *<sup>ilu</sup>* *Marduk*. It is therefore probable that the remaining phrase (<span class="small-caps">DINGIR</span>) <span class="small-caps">EN-LIL DA-GA-NI</span> = [ . . . ]*-mu* *<sup>ilu</sup>* *Bēl*, which may perhaps be restored as [*še*]*-mu* *<sup>ilu</sup>* *Bēl* (see below, *sub* No. 94).
[^fn-p0264-2]: (4) <span class="small-caps">DUR-KA</span> is the Sumerian equivalent of *ṣirratu* (No. 94, l. 28, and No. 95, l. 14); the exact meaning of the word is uncertain, though it is clearly either an object, or a symbol, of power.
[^fn-p0265-1]: (1) The preceding inscription, which also records the building of E-babbar by Ḫammurabi, was probably deposited in the temple. The bricks on which the above inscription is found were built into its walls.
[^fn-p0267-1]: (1) For the meaning of this sign, see above, p. 181, note 2.
[^fn-p0267-2]: (2) The sign is clearly <span class="small-caps">dul</span> (= *katāmu*).
[^fn-p0267-3]: (3) See above, p. 182, note 3.
[^fn-p0269-1]: (1) In ll. 10–17 Ḫammurabi describes his relations to the four gods Anu, Bēl, Šamaš, and Marduk, and in No. 59, ll. 7–10, he describes his relations to Bēl, Ninib, Šamaš, and Marduk. Of the expressions employed in the two passages, *mi-gir* *<sup>ilu</sup>* *Bēl* (No. 59) corresponds to *mi-gi-ir* *<sup>ilu</sup>* *Šamaš*, and *ri-’-u na-ram* *<sup>ilu</sup>* *Ninib* (No. 59) corresponds to *rē’ū na-ra-am* *<sup>ilu</sup>* *Marduk*. It is therefore not unlikely that *še-mu-u* *<sup>ilu</sup>* *Šamaš* (No. 59) corresponds to [ . . . . ]*-mu* *<sup>ilu</sup>* *Bēl*. Moreover, the break at the beginning of the line only admits of the restoration of a small character such as *še*.
[^fn-p0273-1]: (1) That is, “The abundance of the people.”
[^fn-p0273-2]: (2) The verb probably implies “to change, to convert,” not “to restore, to turn back into.”
[^fn-p0273-3]: (3) Or “wall.”
[^fn-p0275-1]: (1) The copy is on clay, and was made in the Neo-Babylonian period.
[^fn-p0276-1]: (1) The text is inscribed upon a clay tablet, and was copied from an ancient inscription which was probably engraved upon stone and must have resembled the stone tablets of Samsu-iluna (see below, p. 199). The original from which the copy was made was in bad condition, for the scribe has left his third column blank with the exception of two notes in smaller characters, *ḫi-b*[*i*] and *ḫi-bu-u*, i.e. “broken, destroyed.” Like No. VIII the copy was made in the Neo-Babylonian period.

## VIII. INSCRIPTIONS REFERRING TO ḪAMMURABI

### VIII.<br />INSCRIPTIONS REFERRING TO<br />ḪAMMURABI.

#### **I.**<br />VOTIVE INSCRIPTION IN SUMERIAN.

[Brit. Mus., No. 22,454; pl. 126, No. 66.]

##### <span class="small-caps">Transliteration.</span>

COL. I. [ . . . . . ] *ra - tum*  
E - GI - A[^fn-p0277-1] <span class="small-caps">An</span> NA

*Ḫa - am - mu - r[a - bi]*

15 *ra - bi - a - an* (ID) [ . . . . . ] 15

20 IGI - E - DI - NE N[E - GIN GIN] 20

#### <span class="small-caps">Translation.</span>

For [ . . . ]ratum,[^fn-p0278-1] the bride of Anu, who hath attained to (?) dominion, the lady of strength and abundance,[^fn-p0278-2] (the lady of ?) the mountain,[^fn-p0278-3] (whose worship) is carefully tended, the merciful lady, who for her spouse maketh favourable her exalted word, for his lady, on behalf of the life of Ḫammurabi, the king of Martu, Ibirum[ . . . . ], the *rabianu* of the (district of the) river [ . . . . ], the son of Šuban[ . . . ], hath marvellously fashioned[^fn-p0278-4] a guardian image[^fn-p0278-5] worthy of her divinity, for her service (?) within the dwelling which is beloved of her.

### II.<br />FRAGMENT OF A SUMERIAN INSCRIPTION.

[Brit. Mus., No. N. 1,667; pl. 128, No. 67.]

#### <span class="small-caps">Transliteration.</span>

> [ . . . . . . . . . . ]  
> [ . . ] - <span class="small-caps">ne - ag -</span> [ . . . ]  
> [<span class="small-caps">a</span>]<span class="small-caps">r - ku - mu -</span> [ . . . . ]  
> [ . . . ] - <span class="small-caps">li - ne - a</span>[<span class="small-caps">g -</span> . . . ]  
> 5 [ . . . ] <span class="small-caps">ba - ra - mu -</span> [ . . . ] 5  
> <span class="small-caps">e -</span> [ . . . ]  
> *Ḫa - am - mu -* [*ra - bi*]  
> <span class="small-caps">nam lugal -</span> [<span class="small-caps">a</span> . . . . ]  
> <span class="small-caps">kalam - ma pa ud - d</span>[<span class="small-caps">u</span> . . . ]  
> 10 <span class="small-caps">ag - a - me - e</span>[<span class="small-caps">n</span>] 10  
> <span class="small-caps">ud</span> (<span class="small-caps">dingir</span>)<span class="small-caps">en - lil -</span> [ . . . . ]  
> [ . . . . . . . . . . ]

[Both the beginning and the end of the column are wanting.]

#### <span class="small-caps">Summary.</span>

These few words from an inscription referring to Ḫammurabi are engraved upon a fragment of basalt. The fragment is some three inches in thickness, and probably formed part of a statue. It is possible that we should translate ll. 7 ff. as “I, Ḫammurabi, am the creator of the kingdom . . . . and the land,” in which case we may conclude that the fragment is part of a statue of Ḫammurabi himself.

<span class="small-caps">Rev.</span> . . . . on the bank of the river Araḫ[ . . . ], did Ḫammurabi, the *adda* [ . . . ], build for the preservation of his own life . . . .[^fn-p0280-1]

### Notes

[^fn-p0277-1]: (1) There is probably no sign wanting after <span class="small-caps">a</span>; if the marks are traces of a character, <span class="small-caps">dingir</span> is possible, though Anu’s name is usually written without the determinative.
[^fn-p0278-1]: (1) We should expect here the name of the deity in whose honour the inscription was set up, and who from l. 2 is clearly the goddess Anatu. If the name or title is Semitic, as its form suggests, it may possibly be restored as [*šar*]-*ra-tum*, “queen”; the preposition *ana* is necessarily absent, as the inscription itself is Sumerian, and the postposition <span class="small-caps">ir</span> occurs in l. 10.
[^fn-p0278-2]: (2) In Semitic *bēlit kuzbi u ulṣi*.
[^fn-p0278-3]: (3) It is not clear whether <span class="small-caps">ḫar-sag ga</span> is to be taken by itself or with the lines which precede or follow it; perhaps it should be translated “my mountain,” a phrase descriptive of the goddess’s strength.
[^fn-p0278-4]: (4) In Semitic *ana tabrāti ušālik*.
[^fn-p0278-5]: (5) It may be inferred that the flat slab on which the inscription is engraved did not form part of the <span class="small-caps">alad</span>, or “guardian image,” but was set up in the temple of the goddess as a record of Ibirum’s piety. On the slab beside the inscription is a figure in relief, a photograph of which has been reproduced as the frontispiece to the present volume; we may probably see in this figure a representation of Ḫammurabi (see above, Introduction).
[^fn-p0280-1]: (1) The text is inscribed upon a clay tablet, and is probably a Neo-Babylonian copy made from an older original. So little has been preserved that it is not clear in what connection Sumula-ilu and Samsu-iluna are mentioned on the obverse. The reverse refers to the building of a wall, or fortress, on the banks of a river; from l. 6 it may be inferred that the building was dedicated to a god or goddess.

#### <span class="small-caps">Translation.</span>

When Anu and Bēl, the king of heaven and earth, looked with joy upon Marduk, the first-born son of Ea, and gave unto him the dominion over the four quarters of the world and called him by an exalted name among the Anunnaki, and established for him the foundation of Babylon like unto heaven and earth, then Marduk, the lord of his land, who is endowed (?) with wisdom, unto me, Samsu-iluna, the king of his abundance, granted the whole of the world to rule, and with majesty he commanded me to settle his own land in security, and to rule the scattered peoples in prosperity for ever.

Samsu-iluna, the mighty king, the king of Babylon, the king who hath brought to subjection the four quarters of the world, am I. With mine own power and in my great wisdom (I restored) the fortress of Dūr-Zakar in Nippur for the goddess Nin-maḫ,[^fn-p0287-1] the mother who bore me ; the fortress of Dūr-padda for Rammān, my helper ; the fortress Dūr-lagaba for Sin, the god who begat me ; the fortress Dūr-Iabugani for Lugal-diri-tugab, who hath increased my kingdom ; the fortresses of Dūr-Gula-dūru and Dūr-uṣi-ana-Urra for Nergal, who hath overthrown[^fn-p0287-2] mine enemies. These six strong fortresses, which Sumula-ilu, my mighty father, the fifth father of my father, had built, in their old age through their own accord had fallen into ruins. In two months I rebuilt their brickwork, I constructed them majestically, I raised their summits like unto a mountain.[^fn-p0288-1]

I have firmly stablished the foundations of all lands, I have exalted the name of Babylon, I have made it great in the four quarters of the world. The fear of the majesty of my kingdom hath covered the bounds of heaven and earth. The great gods with their bright regard beheld this thing, and they bestowed these gifts upon me : (to live) a life which is renewed month by month like the Moon-god ; to wield for ever a peaceful sway over the four quarters of the world ; to attain the desire of my heart like unto a god ; and to walk each day with head raised in joy and gladness of heart.

#### <span class="small-caps">Summary.</span>

The inscription is broken, but from what remains of the reverse it may be inferred that it recorded the building of a wall or fortress. The obverse contains the name and titles of Ammiditana, and may be translated as follows:— “Ammiditana, the mighty king, the king of Babylon, the king of Sumēr and Akkad, the king of the . . . . . of Martu, am I. The [ . . . . . ] descendant of Sumula-ilu, the valiant son of Abēšu’, am I. One who tendereth obedience unto Bēl [ . . . . . am I].” The colophon states that the clay tablet on which the text is written was “(the property) of Bēl-ušallim, the son of [ . . . . . ]bi, the magician.” The tablet was written in the Neo-Babylonian period, but the text was copied from an older original.[^fn-p0290-1]

##### <span class="small-caps">SUMMARY.</span>

This is a votive inscription containing a dedication to the Sun-god, who is described in the opening words of the text as “Šamaš, the great lord of the gods, the king of the temple E-dikud.” The inscription was made by order of “Gimil-Marduk the judge, the son of Mini-Šamaš,” for the preservation of the life of “Ammizaduga, the mighty king, the king of Babylon, his king.” The text, which is on a clay tablet, is not written in the usual cursive hand, but in the more complex and careful character employed in inscriptions upon stone. It probably formed the rough draft used by the engraver when preparing the stone tablet which was intended by Gimil-Marduk to be placed before Šamaš in E-dikud, his temple in Babylon.

[A: Bu. 91-5-9, 284 (pl. 217, No. 101).  B: Brit. Mus., No. 16,924  
(pl. 228, No. 102).]

#### <span class="small-caps">Translation.</span>

##### I. REIGN OF SUMU-ABU.

<span class="small-caps">year.</span>

- 1. [The year in which Sumu-abu became king . . . . ].[^fn-p0294-1]
- 2. [The year in which . . . . . . . . ].
- 3. The year in which the wall[^fn-p0294-2] of [ . . . . ] was built.
- 4. The year in which the temple of Nin-sinna was built.
- 5. The year in which the great temple of Nannar was built.
- 6. The year after that in which the great temple of Nannar was built.
- 7. The second year after that in which the great temple of Nannar was built.
- 8. The year in which the great door of cedar was made for the temple of Nannar.

<span class="small-caps">MU</span> *Ḫa-mu-ra-bi* <span class="small-caps">NIG-SI-DI GAR-RA</span>, “The year in which Ḫammurabi established righteousness.” The fuller formula upon V.A.Th. 1,468 f. (Meissner, No. 49) is probably a variant of the same date ; it reads <span class="small-caps">MU</span> *Ḫa-mu-ra-bi* <span class="small-caps">LUGAL-E ŠAG-GA KALAM-MA NAM-SI-DI</span>, “The year in which Ḫammurabi (established) the heart of the land in righteousness.” We may perhaps see in the title of this year a reference to the reforms undertaken by Ḫammurabi at the beginning of his reign.
